565	\caption{\textbf{Left: The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA (blue) beats full fine-tuning (red) by $12$--$79\%$ on all 6 datasets. \textbf{Right: Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} validated} ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts where raw routing helps; Traffic and Solar are boundary cases (see text).}
566	\label{fig:frozen_paradox}
567	\label{fig:signal_ratio}
568	\end{figure}
569	
570	\textbf{Cross-backbone generalization.}\label{sec:cross_backbone} MOMENT-small/large (RevIN): \textbf{all 18} win ($27$--$81\%$). Moirai (LayerNorm): \textbf{18/18} ($3$--$31\%$). Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} (native sparse MoE): \textbf{all 6 datasets} ($-37\%$ to $-90\%$; Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}), confirming adapter-level routing stacks with backbone-internal routing. \textbf{Negative controls}---Chronos and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} (no instance norm): no collapse (entropy $1.48$--$1.61$), RR-MoA provides no advantage (\textbf{4/18}, within chance)---exactly as Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_backbone}).
571	
572	\textbf{Sparse routing, multi-horizon, and task generalization.}\label{sec:topk}
573	Top-2 adds $4.3$\,ms ($+10\%$); $K{\in}\{3,5,7,10,15,20\}$ changes MSE ${\leq}5\%$, while AdaMix at $K{=}15{,}20$ maintains high entropy ($2.4$--$2.7$) but MSE remains $2$--$6{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA---confirming routing failure is informational, not capacity-limited. RR-MoA wins \textbf{24/24} horizons ($H{\in}\{96{-}720\}$), generalizes to imputation ($7/8$ datasets, $-44\%$ to $-77\%$) and classification ($86.5\%$ on BasicMotions; Appendix~\ref{app:topk},~\ref{app:imputation}).
574	
575	\section{Conclusion}

372	\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, trained from scratch) achieves lower absolute MSE---as do PatchTST and iTransformer (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows). The diagnosis predicts that providing experts with raw-signal access should close this gap, since the frozen-backbone normalization cascade destroys the very statistics that drive DLinear's strength. We evaluate three architectures of increasing sophistication: \textbf{dual-stream} (learnable scalar $\alpha_k$ blends raw and backbone branches), \textbf{multi-resolution} (concatenate raw + pooled hidden features), and \textbf{Residual-IA} (raw branch is the primary predictor; backbone contributes an additive residual scaled by a per-sample instance-adaptive gate):

Pooled $108/123$ across six backbones ($65$ sig wins vs $11$ losses; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}).}

394	{\looseness=-1 Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} achieves \textbf{$108/123$ match-or-beat} across six backbones, six datasets, and four horizons ($21/24$ MOMENT-small, $87/99$ cross-backbone including Moirai-MoE $15/15$; Tables~\ref{tab:residual_ia_plus_backbone}--\ref{tab:residual_ia_plus_mhcb}). A $1{,}025$-run diagnostic sweep (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) identified gate-initialization pathology ($\sigma(0){=}0.5$ plateau); four fixes resolve it: residual formulation, gate-init $b{=}{-}2$, warmup, and shared NLinear raw branch matching DLinear's $49$K param budget. \textit{The remaining gap on MOMENT is predicted by our diagnosis}: RevIN strips the statistics DLinear exploits, making $R(\mathcal{D})$ a \emph{TSFM value-add indicator}. On Timer-XL (no RevIN), Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} achieves \textbf{$12/12$}.}

253	\textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). This should be read as ``a frozen 5-expert MoE beats an unfrozen single-head adapter''---the single-head PEFT heuristic still holds ($1.254{\to}1.060$, $-15\%$). Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. Extended FT (50 epochs, cosine schedule) closes the gap by only ${\sim}4\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).
254	
255	\begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
256	\label{prop:frozen}
257	Consider a $2$-expert MoE with backbone $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$, router $p{=}\sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{h})$, loss $\ell{=}p\,\ell_1{+}(1{-}p)\,\ell_2$. Under gradient descent on $(A,\mathbf{w})$: \textup{(i)} the dominant expert's loss decreases faster ($\dot{\ell}_k \propto {-}p_k\|\nabla_\mathbf{h}\ell_k\|^2$), creating self-reinforcing collapse toward $p{\in}\{0,1\}$; \textup{(ii)} freezing $A$ breaks this loop (proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}).
258	\end{proposition}
259	
260	\textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms the mechanism in a full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. \textbf{Routing vs.\ ensembling:} uniform mixture ($w_k{=}1/K$) is $30$--$75\%$ worse; independent ensembles (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) $37$--$46\%$ worse---learned per-sample routing is the source of gains.
261

Extended grid in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}.}
265	\label{tab:adamix}

292	\textbf{AdaMix} collapses to a single expert whenever backbone layers are unfrozen: entropy $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configs (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}), below $0.55$ in the other 5. \textbf{Controlled RevIN ablation.} The green rows show AdaMix with RevIN \emph{disabled}: entropy recovers from $0.000$ to $0.66$--$1.32$---same backbone, same unfreezing, only RevIN removed---proving normalization is the causal mechanism. BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm produce the same collapse (Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization}). \textbf{The causal contrast is sharpest on Timer-XL} (Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}): under identical last-4 unfreezing, MOMENT entropy crashes to $0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps while Timer-XL stays at $1.60$ for all $400$ steps---opposite trajectories with the same code, isolating RevIN as the mechanism.

Unfrozen with RevIN \emph{disabled} (green dashed) also remains stable ($0.82$ at step 400), confirming RevIN is the causal mechanism. 1193	
1194	\textbf{Spectral-temporal router (FFT-Router).} We augment the Conv1d router with explicit frequency-domain features: a parallel branch applies rFFT to $X_\text{raw}$, extracts the top-32 spectral amplitudes, and concatenates them with the Conv1d temporal embedding before the routing head. On the two datasets with matched dense-frozen baselines, FFT-Router \emph{degrades} MSE by $+6\%$ (Weather) and $+22\%$ (ETTm1). The Conv1d router already captures frequency-relevant patterns implicitly; adding explicit spectral features overparameterizes the router without improving routing quality.
1195	
1196	\textbf{Statistic-aware information bottleneck (SAIB).} An auxiliary loss forces the router's penultimate representation to predict the window-level $[\mu, \sigma]$---directly operationalizing Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}. With SAIB coefficients $\lambda \in \{0.1, 1.0\}$, MSE is unchanged on ETTm1 ($0.395$ vs $0.397$ baseline) and slightly degraded on Weather ($0.250$ vs $0.236$). Routing entropy increases to $1.55$ (near-maximum $\log 5 = 1.61$), indicating the auxiliary loss disperses routing weights without improving forecast accuracy. This confirms the Conv1d router already encodes $\mu, \sigma$ without explicit supervision.
1197	
1198	\textbf{Instance-adaptive gating (IA-Gating).} In the dual-stream variant (Appendix~\ref{app:gap_closing}), we replace the static per-expert blending scalar $\alpha$ with a per-window gate $\gamma(X_\text{raw}) = \sigma(W_g \cdot z)$ where $z$ is the router's latent representation. Across 6 datasets (frozen, 3 seeds), IA-Gating wins on 4/6 (ETTh2 $-8.0\%$, ETTm2 $-6.4\%$, ETTm1 $-1.9\%$, Electricity $-0.4\%$) and loses on 2/6 (Weather $+6.1\%$, ETTh1 $+0.3\%$). The learned gates cluster tightly around $\gamma \approx 0.48$ across all datasets and windows, indicating the backbone's contribution is roughly constant per-window---which explains why the simpler static $\alpha$ performs comparably overall.
1199	
1200	\section{Extended RR-MoA Freeze Grid}
1201	\label{app:extended_rrmoa}
1202

1174	\section{Routing Ablations}
1175	\label{app:routing_ablations}
1176	
1177	\textbf{What the router learns.} Per-sample routing decisions correlate with amplitude and volatility---the very statistics RevIN strips (Figure~\ref{fig:routing_viz}). On ETTh1, Expert~0 (mean-pool) serves as the generalist ($53\%$), while Expert~2 (max-pool) handles low-amplitude windows and Expert~3 (attention-pool) handles high-amplitude windows. The router reserves specialized processing for windows at the distribution tails, empirically validating the conditional independence assumption $E \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid (M,\Sigma)$ of Part~(iii) of Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}.
1178	
1179	\begin{figure}[h]
1180	\centering
1181	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/routing_viz.pdf}
1182	\caption{\textbf{Per-sample expert assignment vs.\ input statistics} (ETTh1, seed 42, $N{=}5{,}000$ test windows). Left: amplitude vs.\ volatility; right: mean level vs.\ amplitude. Colors indicate dominant expert (top-1 routing weight). Expert selection clusters by amplitude and volatility---the very statistics RevIN strips---empirically validating the conditional independence assumption $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S$ from Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(iii).}
1183	\label{fig:routing_viz}
1184	\end{figure}
1185	
1186	\textbf{Regime-specific adaptation.} Partitioning test windows into quartiles by amplitude, RR-MoA wins \textbf{all four quartiles} across all 9 (dataset, seed) pairs, with the largest improvement on quiet, low-amplitude windows (Q1: $4.6{\times}$ better, $0.24$ vs $1.09$ on ETTh1) and the smallest on volatile, high-amplitude windows (Q4: $1.3{\times}$, $1.54$ vs $1.97$).
1187	
1188	\textbf{Sufficient statistic router (SSR).} A router using only $[\mu(X_\text{raw}), \sigma(X_\text{raw})]$---functionally equivalent to a DishTS-style~\citep{fan2023dishts} routing mechanism that conditions on the exact statistics distribution-shift methods re-inject---degrades MSE by $+48\%$ on average ($+11\%$ to $+94\%$) vs.\ the Conv1d router across all 8 forecasting datasets. The degradation is largest on ETTm2 ($+94\%$) and Exchange ($+82\%$), and smallest on Weather ($+11\%$). While Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} correctly identifies the \emph{direction} of the information loss, effective routing requires temporal shape patterns beyond location-scale statistics.
1189	
1190	\textbf{Temporal-shuffle ablation.} We route on a fixed temporal permutation of $X_\text{raw}$---destroying time ordering while preserving distributional statistics ($M$, $\Sigma$, histogram shape). Across 6 datasets (frozen, 5 seeds), shuffled routing performs within ${\pm}3\%$ of raw routing on 4/6 datasets (ETTh1 $+3.2\%$, ETTh2 $+1.2\%$, ETTm1 $-1.2\%$, Electricity $+0.7\%$), with Weather improving $-8.5\%$ and ETTm2 improving $-16.0\%$. Entropy remains high ($1.43$--$1.58$). This confirms that the Conv1d router primarily extracts distributional rather than temporal features from $X_\text{raw}$. Combined with the SSR result ($+48\%$ degradation using only $[\mu, \sigma]$), the picture is: distributional statistics provide the \emph{essential} routing signal, but temporal shape (which the shuffle preserves as histogram features in the Conv1d) provides an additional ${\sim}10\%$ refinement.
1191	
1192	\textbf{Router-Detached Gradient Flow (RDGF).} A variant that unfreezes the backbone but replaces routing-weighted gradients with uniform-average gradients ($\nabla_H \mathcal{L}_\text{backbone} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_k \nabla_H \mathcal{L}_k$) degrades MSE by $+35\%$ to $+100\%$ across three datasets. This confirms the Frozen Paradox is structural.
1193

diff --git a/main.tex b/main.tex
index ac10c4e..abb07d3 100644
--- a/main.tex
+++ b/main.tex
@@ -51,7 +51,7 @@
 \maketitle
 
 \begin{abstract}
-We discover a fundamental incompatibility between instance normalization and mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing on time series foundation models (TSFMs): routing entropy drops to $0.000{\pm}0.000$, yet the failure is not entropy collapse---a normalization dose-response reveals that entropy stays high while MSE degrades $283\%$ (phase transition at full normalization). The cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: RevIN strips per-window mean and scale that the router needs for discriminative gating. Seven converging controls isolate this, including opposite entropy trajectories on Timer-XL (no RevIN, entropy \emph{rises} $1.49{\to}1.60$) and a 720-run rescue sweep where all eleven standard mechanisms fail ($2.7{\times}$ worse). A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) predicts which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$), including where it does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). The diagnosis is precise enough that the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on raw pre-normalization input while the backbone stays frozen. RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$) on the primary backbone, generalizes across four additional backbones (two negative controls confirm normalization-specificity), and produces the \textbf{Frozen Paradox}: frozen beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} achieves \textbf{parity-or-better with DLinear on $108/123$ cells} across six backbones (Timer-XL $12/12$; Moirai-MoE $15/15$; $65$ significant wins vs $11$ losses).
+We discover a fundamental incompatibility between instance normalization and mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing on time series foundation models (TSFMs): routing entropy drops to $0.000{\pm}0.000$, yet the failure is not entropy collapse---a normalization dose-response reveals that entropy stays high while MSE degrades $283\%$ (phase transition at full normalization). The cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: RevIN strips per-window mean and scale that the router needs for discriminative gating. Seven converging causal controls isolate this---most strikingly, opposite entropy trajectories under identical unfreezing (Timer-XL rises $1.49{\to}1.60$ while MOMENT collapses $0.60{\to}0.00$)---and all eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms fail ($2.7{\times}$ worse; 720 runs). A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) predicts which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$), including where it does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). The diagnosis is precise enough that the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on raw pre-normalization input while the backbone stays frozen. RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$) on the primary backbone, generalizes across four additional backbones (two negative controls confirm normalization-specificity), and produces the \textbf{Frozen Paradox}: frozen beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} achieves \textbf{parity-or-better with DLinear on $108/123$ cells} across six backbones (Timer-XL $12/12$; Moirai-MoE $15/15$; $65$ significant wins vs $11$ losses).
 \end{abstract}
 
 \section{Introduction}
@@ -65,7 +65,7 @@ The transformer architecture~\citep{vaswani2017attention} has enabled foundation
 
 \textbf{MoE on TSFMs collapses.} When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to a TSFM with RevIN, routing entropy drops to $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configs (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: the dominant expert receives larger gradients, reshaping the backbone to serve it better in a feedback loop within ${\sim}50$ steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). Even when frozen, hidden-state routing underperforms because RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the statistics the router needs. RevIN applied to the router degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}); Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} (no RevIN) does \emph{not} collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}), confirming the failure is normalization-specific.
 
-\textbf{Our fix.} We propose \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}: the router reads the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while the backbone is kept strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The fix's simplicity is a direct consequence of the diagnosis's precision---the intervention is unambiguous once the cause is isolated. Without it, practitioners exhaust the full optimization-side toolkit without success (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}).
+\textbf{Our fix.} We propose \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}: the router reads the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while the backbone is kept strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The fix's simplicity is a direct consequence of the diagnosis's precision---the intervention is unambiguous once the cause is isolated, whereas 11 optimization-side mechanisms all fail (720 runs; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}).
 
 Our contributions:
 
@@ -167,7 +167,7 @@ Our contributions:
 
 \subsection{Problem Formulation and Expert Pool}
 
-An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states to forecasts, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}).
+An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states to forecasts, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}). Diversity matters (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).
 
 \textbf{Standard protocol.} Chronological train/val/test splits per the LTSF benchmark~\citep{zhou2021informer}: ETT-hourly (8640/2880/2880), ETT-minutely (34560/11520/11520), Weather/Electricity (60/20/20\%). Final evaluation uses the held-out test set.
 
@@ -221,7 +221,7 @@ where $G_\psi$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($\sim$1.1K params)
 \label{sec:architectures}
 \label{sec:freeze_ablation}
 
-Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} presents the central experimental result of the paper. Using the \emph{same five expert adapter heads} (mean, last-token, max, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool) in all rows, we compare Top-2 RR-MoA against the single best fixed adapter baseline. The full grid is 6 datasets $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ \textbf{54 configurations}; Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} shows the 3 representative datasets in the main text, and Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa_extended} (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}) tabulates ETTh2, ETTm2, and Electricity. \textbf{RR-MoA wins 54 out of 54 configurations} with tight standard deviations.

-\textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. Extended FT (50 epochs, cosine schedule) closes the gap by only ${\sim}4\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).
+{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). Extended FT (50 epochs, cosine) closes the gap by only ${\sim}4\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).}
 
 \begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
 \label{prop:frozen}
 Consider a $2$-expert MoE with backbone $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$, router $p{=}\sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{h})$, loss $\ell{=}p\,\ell_1{+}(1{-}p)\,\ell_2$. Under gradient descent on $(A,\mathbf{w})$: \textup{(i)} the dominant expert's loss decreases faster ($\dot{\ell}_k \propto {-}p_k\|\nabla_\mathbf{h}\ell_k\|^2$), creating self-reinforcing collapse toward $p{\in}\{0,1\}$; \textup{(ii)} freezing $A$ breaks this loop (proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}).
 \end{proposition}
 
-\textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms the mechanism in a full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. \textbf{Routing vs.\ ensembling:} uniform mixture ($w_k{=}1/K$) is $30$--$75\%$ worse; independent ensembles (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) $37$--$46\%$ worse---learned per-sample routing is the source of gains.
+{\looseness=-1 \textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms the mechanism in a full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. Uniform mixture ($w_k{=}1/K$) is $30$--$75\%$ worse; independent ensembles (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) $37$--$46\%$ worse---learned per-sample routing is the source of gains.}

+\textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass.} Replacing the router's raw input with RevIN-normalized input (same bypass, stripped statistics) degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ while entropy stays high ($1.50$--$1.57$; Table~\ref{tab:router_input}, Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}), confirming the router relies on the \emph{content} of the stripped statistics, not merely the bypass path.
 
 {\looseness=-1 \textbf{Normalization dose-response.} Interpolating $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\,\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ reveals a \emph{phase transition} (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; 150 runs). MSE degrades mildly for $\alpha{\leq}0.75$ ($+5.4\%$) but catastrophically at $\alpha{=}1.0$ ($+283\%$). Entropy \emph{does not collapse}---it stays in $[1.43, 1.57]$ at all $\alpha$: normalization renders routing uninformative without reducing diversity. A \textbf{temporal-shuffle ablation} (time-permuted raw input) performs within ${\pm}3\%$ of raw on $4/6$ datasets, confirming routing depends on $(M,\Sigma)$ statistics, not temporal ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).}

The signal ratio $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M) + \mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ serves as a monotone proxy for $I(M,\Sigma; E)$: high-$R$ datasets have large routing information relative to $\varepsilon$, producing large raw-routing gains; low-$R$ datasets have $I(M,\Sigma; E) \approx \varepsilon$, producing no gain---validated at $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ across nine datasets.

The bound~(ii) is informative when $I(M,\Sigma; E) \gg \varepsilon$. The signal ratio $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M) + \mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ serves as a monotone proxy for the \emph{potential} routing information in the stripped statistics: high-$R$ datasets have large routing information relative to $\varepsilon$, producing large raw-routing gains; low-$R$ datasets have $I(M,\Sigma; E) \approx \varepsilon$, producing no gain---validated at $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ across nine datasets.\footnote{Direct MI estimation via KNN yields $\rho{=}{-}0.47$; $R(\mathcal{D})$ outperforms it because the variance proxy is robust to the discrete structure of expert assignments ($K{=}5$).}

 \textbf{Sparse routing, multi-horizon, and task generalization.}\label{sec:topk}
 Top-2 adds $4.3$\,ms ($+10\%$); $K{\in}\{3,5,7,10,15,20\}$ changes MSE ${\leq}5\%$, while AdaMix at $K{=}15{,}20$ maintains high entropy ($2.4$--$2.7$) but MSE remains $2$--$6{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA---confirming routing failure is informational, not capacity-limited.
